========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today, I really appreciate your time. I, um... I hope it's okay that I'm here. I know other people have much bigger problems, so I don't want to make a fuss. 

But I came in because... well, I've been having a little trouble with my thoughts lately. Once a worry starts for me—whether it's about my mother getting up in the middle of the night, or a grant report I'm putting together, or just wondering if I missed a small detail at work—I just can't seem to... shelve it in my mind. If that makes sense? 

I try really hard to manage it. I make lots of lists to stay organized, and I try my best to redirect myself, but no matter how hard I try, the worry just stays stuck there. I've been trying to just push past it so I don't bother anyone, but it's getting a little exhausting. Am I explaining that okay?

Well, um... I guess... I mean, I really try not to let it show. I want to be a good employee and a good leader for my team. But, um... lately, I suppose it has been getting a little harder to keep all the plates spinning, so to speak.

Oh, um, physical health... well, I really try not to complain about little aches and pains. Everyone gets those, right? But, um, I suppose sleep has been a little... tricky lately.

Oh, um... my relationships? Well, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be as supportive and present as I can be for everyone. I'd hate to think my worrying is being a burden on anyone else.

Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these worries that you can't seem to shelve in your mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, I like how you phrased that, about shelving them. Um... well, if I really think about it, it’s been about a year and two months. \n\nThat's, um, that's when my mother moved into my home. She had a bit of a fall, you see, and... well, it just made sense for her to come stay with me. I love having her, I really do! I hope it doesn't sound like I'm complaining about taking care of her, because I'm absolutely not. It's a privilege to help her. But I suppose... I guess that's around the time my mind really started working overtime, just trying to make sure she's safe and everything is perfectly taken care of for her. \n\nIs that... does that timeline make sense for what you're looking for? I hope I gave you the right kind of answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or difficulty sleeping, since your worrying started?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, physical health... well, I really try not to complain about little aches and pains. Everyone gets those, right? But, um, I suppose sleep has been a little... tricky lately. \n\nIt's just that, um, most nights I end up lying there for a while, kind of replaying the whole day in my head, and then... well, trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up the next day. Just to be prepared, you know? I want to make sure I'm doing a good job. And then, if I happen to wake up and hear my mother moving around in the night, I just want to make sure she's okay, so I'll listen, and... then I might be up for an hour or two after that. \n\nI still make it to work on time every single morning! I really pride myself on being punctual. It's just that I usually arrive at the library feeling like I... well, like I never really rested. But I just drink an extra cup of tea and push through it. I hope that's normal? I mean, I know a lot of people have trouble sleeping sometimes. Am I giving you the kind of details you need?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you more distress than it is interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really thoughtful question. I guess... well, like I brought up earlier, I suppose it really is interfering, isn't it? Having to hand off that grant presentation because I froze, and missing those little details at the library... that definitely gets in the way of my day-to-day work. Which is just so hard for me to admit, because I always want to be perfectly reliable for my staff. \n\nBut I think... I think the interference is actually what's causing the distress? Because I just worry so much now about whether I'm still capable of leading my team well, and it really upsets me to think I might be letting them down. I hope I'm not sounding too dramatic about it all. I just want to be a good leader. Does that... does that answer what you were asking? I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or things you've been avoiding because you worry about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... avoiding things? Well, I don't think so. I really pride myself on being dependable. \n\nI suppose... well, I still go to work every day, and I still take care of my mother, and I still do the grocery shopping and all the normal things you're supposed to do. I might, um, I might double-check things a bit more than I used to, or maybe I take a little longer to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm making the right choice... but I wouldn't call that avoiding, right? That's just being careful. \n\nI hope that's a good answer? I just don't think there's anything specific I'm staying away from. Is that... is that the kind of information you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my relationships? Well, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be as supportive and present as I can be for everyone. I'd hate to think my worrying is being a burden on anyone else. \n\nMy mother and I still get along just fine, of course. I mean, I might ask her a few extra times if she's locked the doors or if she's feeling okay, but she knows I just care about her. And my friends... well, I haven't seen them quite as much lately, but that's just because work has been so busy, and I've been so focused on making sure everything at the library is running smoothly. It's not because of the worrying or anything. When we do talk, I always make sure to listen to their problems and be a good friend. \n\nI really try to keep my stress to myself so I don't bother anyone with it. Oh, um... judged by others? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I try very hard to be a good colleague and a good friend, so I'd like to think people don't have a reason to judge me negatively. I always make sure to do my job well at the library and be as helpful as I can.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... interfering with my daily life? Well, I really try my hardest not to let it get in the way of things, especially at work. I pride myself on being very reliable. 

But I suppose... well, maybe it's a little bit of both? The emotional part is certainly tiring, but... I have noticed a few little hiccups at work lately. It's a bit embarrassing to admit, actually. I've started missing some small details at the library. Just minor things, really, but they are important, and I usually never miss them. 

And... well, there was a grant presentation recently. I actually sort of froze up during it, which is just so unlike me. I ended up having to hand off parts of it to someone else because I just couldn't find my words. I felt terrible about it. I really want to be a good leader for my team, and that little incident has made me worry about whether I can keep leading them well. 

I just hope my colleagues don't think I'm slacking off or anything. I really am trying my best. Do you think... is it common for stress to make you freeze up like that? I just want to make sure I'm not letting anyone down.

I really want to be a good leader for my team, and that little incident has made me worry about whether I can keep leading them well. \n\nI just hope my colleagues don't think I'm slacking off or anything. I really am trying my best. Do you think... is it common for stress to make you freeze up like that? I just want to make sure I'm not letting anyone down.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you afraid of any specific objects, situations, or activities that triggers your worrying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... specific objects or activities? No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not afraid of anything like spiders or heights or... flying, or anything like that. It's nothing specific that I avoid. I still go everywhere I need to go and do all my normal activities.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today, I really appreciate your time. I, um... I hope it's okay that I'm here. I know other people have much bigger problems, so I don't want to make a fuss. \n\nBut I came in because... well, I've been having a little trouble with my thoughts lately. Once a worry starts for me—whether it's about my mother getting up in the middle of the night, or a grant report I'm putting together, or just wondering if I missed a small detail at work—I just can't seem to... shelve it in my mind. If that makes sense? \n\nI try really hard to manage it. I make lots of lists to stay organized, and I try my best to redirect myself, but no matter how hard I try, the worry just stays stuck there. I've been trying to just push past it so I don't bother anyone, but it's getting a little exhausting. Am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or worries?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think I do anything like that. I mean, I like things to be organized, of course—working in a library, you sort of have to be. So I do double-check my lists and make sure everything is in its proper place, but... it's just to make sure I'm doing a good job. I don't have to touch things a certain number of times or anything like that. I really hope that's not what it sounded like I meant. I'm just a little overly thorough sometimes, I think. Is that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no. I don't think so. I really try my best not to let it interfere with my responsibilities. I mean, I still go to work every day at the library, and I still do all my errands and help out my family. \n\nI wouldn't want to inconvenience anyone or... or let them down just because my mind is racing a bit. So I usually just push through it, even if I'm worrying the whole time. I hope that's a good thing? I just try really hard to keep going so I'm not a bother to anyone. Did I answer that right?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been causing you a lot of distress or been interfering with your daily life in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess... I mean, I really try not to let it show. I want to be a good employee and a good leader for my team. But, um... lately, I suppose it has been getting a little harder to keep all the plates spinning, so to speak. \n\nI've, um... I've started missing a few small details at the library. Nothing catastrophic, I promise! Just... important little things that I normally wouldn't miss. And, um, there was this grant presentation recently... I actually had to hand off parts of it to someone else because I just... I sort of froze up. \n\nIt's really not like me at all, and it's so embarrassing to admit. I just worry so much now about whether I'm still capable of leading my team the way they deserve to be led. I really hope my supervisor doesn't think I'm slacking off. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these worries that you can't seem to shelve in your mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, I like how you phrased that, about shelving them. Um... well, if I really think about it, it’s been about a year and two months. \n\nThat's, um, that's when my mother moved into my home. She had a bit of a fall, you see, and... well, it just made sense for her to come stay with me. I love having her, I really do! I hope it doesn't sound like I'm complaining about taking care of her, because I'm absolutely not. It's a privilege to help her. But I suppose... I guess that's around the time my mind really started working overtime, just trying to make sure she's safe and everything is perfectly taken care of for her. \n\nIs that... does that timeline make sense for what you're looking for? I hope I gave you the right kind of answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or difficulty sleeping, since your worrying started?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, physical health... well, I really try not to complain about little aches and pains. Everyone gets those, right? But, um, I suppose sleep has been a little... tricky lately. \n\nIt's just that, um, most nights I end up lying there for a while, kind of replaying the whole day in my head, and then... well, trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up the next day. Just to be prepared, you know? I want to make sure I'm doing a good job. And then, if I happen to wake up and hear my mother moving around in the night, I just want to make sure she's okay, so I'll listen, and... then I might be up for an hour or two after that. \n\nI still make it to work on time every single morning! I really pride myself on being punctual. It's just that I usually arrive at the library feeling like I... well, like I never really rested. But I just drink an extra cup of tea and push through it. I hope that's normal? I mean, I know a lot of people have trouble sleeping sometimes. Am I giving you the kind of details you need?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you more distress than it is interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really thoughtful question. I guess... well, like I brought up earlier, I suppose it really is interfering, isn't it? Having to hand off that grant presentation because I froze, and missing those little details at the library... that definitely gets in the way of my day-to-day work. Which is just so hard for me to admit, because I always want to be perfectly reliable for my staff. \n\nBut I think... I think the interference is actually what's causing the distress? Because I just worry so much now about whether I'm still capable of leading my team well, and it really upsets me to think I might be letting them down. I hope I'm not sounding too dramatic about it all. I just want to be a good leader. Does that... does that answer what you were asking? I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or things you've been avoiding because you worry about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... avoiding things? Well, I don't think so. I mean, I really try my best to face things head-on. I'm not the type to just run away from responsibilities or anything like that. I really pride myself on being dependable. \n\nI suppose... well, I still go to work every day, and I still take care of my mother, and I still do the grocery shopping and all the normal things you're supposed to do. I might, um, I might double-check things a bit more than I used to, or maybe I take a little longer to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm making the right choice... but I wouldn't call that avoiding, right? That's just being careful. \n\nI hope that's a good answer? I just don't think there's anything specific I'm staying away from. Is that... is that the kind of information you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my relationships? Well, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be as supportive and present as I can be for everyone. I'd hate to think my worrying is being a burden on anyone else. \n\nMy mother and I still get along just fine, of course. I mean, I might ask her a few extra times if she's locked the doors or if she's feeling okay, but she knows I just care about her. And my friends... well, I haven't seen them quite as much lately, but that's just because work has been so busy, and I've been so focused on making sure everything at the library is running smoothly. It's not because of the worrying or anything. When we do talk, I always make sure to listen to their problems and be a good friend. \n\nI really try to keep my stress to myself so I don't bother anyone with it. I hope that's the right way to handle things? I just want to make sure I'm still being a good daughter and a good friend. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worrying has affected your ability to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... enjoying my time with them? I think I still enjoy it. I mean, I really value the time I get to spend with my mother and my friends. They're very important to me. \n\nI suppose... well, sometimes when I'm with them, my mind might wander a little bit to work, or I might be thinking about whether I remembered to send an email, but I always try to snap right back to the conversation. I'd never want them to feel like I wasn't paying attention or that I wasn't happy to be there. I always make sure to smile and be engaged. \n\nSo, I think I still enjoy it just as much. I really try my best to be present. Is that... do you think it's normal to get a little distracted sometimes? I hope I'm not doing anything wrong by having those thoughts pop up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about being judged or evaluated by others, such as your colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... judged by others? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I try very hard to be a good colleague and a good friend, so I'd like to think people don't have a reason to judge me negatively. I always make sure to do my job well at the library and be as helpful as I can. \n\nI suppose... well, I do want people to think well of me, of course. Sometimes I might double-check my work just to be absolutely sure there aren't any mistakes, or I might think over a conversation later to make sure I didn't say anything that could be taken the wrong way. But that's just because I want to be considerate and professional, you know? I wouldn't call it worrying about being evaluated. I just like to be thorough. \n\nI think everyone wants to make a good impression, right? I hope it's okay that I care about doing a good job. Does that sound like a normal way to approach things?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is interfering with your daily life more than it is causing you emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... interfering with my daily life? Well, I really try my hardest not to let it get in the way of things, especially at work. I pride myself on being very reliable. \n\nBut I suppose... well, maybe it's a little bit of both? The emotional part is certainly tiring, but... I have noticed a few little hiccups at work lately. It's a bit embarrassing to admit, actually. I've started missing some small details at the library. Just minor things, really, but they are important, and I usually never miss them. \n\nAnd... well, there was a grant presentation recently. I actually sort of froze up during it, which is just so unlike me. I ended up having to hand off parts of it to someone else because I just couldn't find my words. I felt terrible about it. I really want to be a good leader for my team, and that little incident has made me worry about whether I can keep leading them well. \n\nI just hope my colleagues don't think I'm slacking off or anything. I really am trying my best. Do you think... is it common for stress to make you freeze up like that? I just want to make sure I'm not letting anyone down.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any specific objects, situations, or activities that triggers your worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... specific objects or activities? No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not afraid of anything like spiders or heights or... flying, or anything like that. It's nothing specific that I avoid. I still go everywhere I need to go and do all my normal activities. \n\nMy worries are usually just... well, just about everyday things, like I mentioned with work or making sure I'm taking care of my responsibilities. It's not triggered by a certain object or a particular place. I always try my best to just go about my day normally and get everything done. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? Is that what you meant by specific situations, or did I misunderstand?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is changing the way you think about yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... changing the way I think about myself? I... I don't think so. I mean, I still see myself as the same person I've always been—someone who tries hard, cares about her job, and wants to do the right thing. \n\nI wouldn't say the worry has changed who I am. Maybe I just feel a little more tired lately, but I still believe in being responsible and reliable. I try very hard to hold on to those values. I don't think of myself as a... a broken person or anything like that. I'm just going through a bit of a stressful patch, right? \n\nI really try to keep a positive outlook on things, even when it's tough. Is it normal to just feel like you're the same person, just... carrying a bit more on your shoulders right now? I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... thoughts that keep repeating? Like... like a broken record that I can't turn off? \n\nNo, I... I don't think I experience that. I mean, my mind can definitely get a little busy sometimes, especially when I'm going over my to-do lists to make sure I haven't missed anything at the library. But... I wouldn't say I have thoughts that just get stuck against my will, or anything strange like that. \n\nIf I need to focus on something else, I can usually shift my attention. It's really just the normal, everyday responsibilities I'm trying to manage, not... not upsetting thoughts that I can't push away. \n\nI hope that's alright? I just want to make sure I'm answering your questions correctly and giving you the right information. Am I explaining that okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I really try my best not to let it interfere with my responsibilities. I mean, I still go to work every day at the library, and I still do all my errands and help out my family. \n\nI wouldn't want to inconvenience anyone or... or let them down just because my mind is racing a bit. So I usually just push through it, even if I'm worrying the whole time. I hope that's a good thing? I just try really hard to keep going so I'm not a bother to anyone. Did I answer that right?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been causing you a lot of distress or been interfering with your daily life in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess... I mean, I really try not to let it show. I want to be a good employee and a good leader for my team. But, um... lately, I suppose it has been getting a little harder to keep all the plates spinning, so to speak. \n\nI've, um... I've started missing a few small details at the library. Nothing catastrophic, I promise! Just... important little things that I normally wouldn't miss. And, um, there was this grant presentation recently... I actually had to hand off parts of it to someone else because I just... I sort of froze up. \n\nIt's really not like me at all, and it's so embarrassing to admit. I just worry so much now about whether I'm still capable of leading my team the way they deserve to be led. I really hope my supervisor doesn't think I'm slacking off. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these worries that you can't seem to shelve in your mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, I like how you phrased that, about shelving them. Um... well, if I really think about it, it’s been about a year and two months. \n\nThat's, um, that's when my mother moved into my home. She had a bit of a fall, you see, and... well, it just made sense for her to come stay with me. I love having her, I really do! I hope it doesn't sound like I'm complaining about taking care of her, because I'm absolutely not. It's a privilege to help her. But I suppose... I guess that's around the time my mind really started working overtime, just trying to make sure she's safe and everything is perfectly taken care of for her. \n\nIs that... does that timeline make sense for what you're looking for? I hope I gave you the right kind of answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or difficulty sleeping, since your worrying started?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, physical health... well, I really try not to complain about little aches and pains. I really pride myself on being punctual. It's just that I usually arrive at the library feeling like I... well, like I never really rested. But I just drink an extra cup of tea and push through it. So I usually just push through it, even if I'm worrying the whole time. I hope that's a good thing? I just try really hard to keep going so I'm not a bother to anyone. Did I answer that right?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been causing you a lot of distress or been interfering with your daily life in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess... I mean, I really try not to let it show. I want to be a good employee and a good leader for my team. But, um... lately, I suppose it has been getting a little harder to keep all the plates spinning, so to speak. \n\nI've, um... I've started missing a few small details at the library. Nothing catastrophic, I promise! Just... important little things that I normally wouldn't miss. And, um, there was this grant presentation recently... I actually had to hand off parts of it to someone else because I just... I sort of froze up. \n\nIt's really not like me at all, and it's so embarrassing to admit. I just worry so much now about whether I'm still capable of leading my team the way they deserve to be led. I really hope my supervisor doesn't think I'm slacking off. Does that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these worries that you can't seem to shelve in your mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, I like how you phrased that, about shelving them. Um... well, if I really think about it, it’s been about a year and two months. \n\nThat's, um, that's when my mother moved into my home. She had a bit of a fall, you see, and... well, it just made sense for her to come stay with me. I love having her, I really do! I hope it doesn't sound like I'm complaining about taking care of her, because I'm absolutely not. It's a privilege to help her. But I suppose... I guess that's around the time my mind really started working overtime, just trying to make sure she's safe and everything is perfectly taken care of for her. \n\nIs that... does that timeline make sense for what you're looking for? I hope I gave you the right kind of answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or difficulty sleeping, since your worrying started?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, physical health... well, I really try not to complain about little aches and pains. Everyone gets those, right? But, um, I suppose sleep has been a little... tricky lately. \n\nIt's just that, um, most nights I end up lying there for a while, kind of replaying the whole day in my head, and then... well, trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up the next day. Just to be prepared, you know? I want to make sure I'm doing a good job. And then, if I happen to wake up and hear my mother moving around in the night, I just want to make sure she's okay, so I'll listen, and... then I might be up for an hour or two after that. \n\nI still make it to work on time every single morning! I really pride myself on being punctual. It's just that I usually arrive at the library feeling like I... well, like I never really rested. But I just drink an extra cup of tea and push through it. I hope that's normal? I mean, I know a lot of people have trouble sleeping sometimes. Am I giving you the kind of details you need?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you more distress than it is interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... that's a really thoughtful question. I guess... well, like I brought up earlier, I suppose it really is interfering, isn't it? Having to hand off that grant presentation because I froze, and missing those little details at the library... that definitely gets in the way of my day-to-day work. Which is just so hard for me to admit, because I always want to be perfectly reliable for my staff. \n\nBut I think... I think the interference is actually what's causing the distress? Because I just worry so much now about whether I'm still capable of leading my team well, and it really upsets me to think I might be letting them down. I hope I'm not sounding too dramatic about it all. I just want to be a good leader. Does that... does that answer what you were asking? I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or things you've been avoiding because you worry about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... avoiding things? Well, I don't think so. I mean, I really try my best to face things head-on. I'm not the type to just run away from responsibilities or anything like that. I really pride myself on being dependable. \n\nI suppose... well, I still go to work every day, and I still take care of my mother, and I still do the grocery shopping and all the normal things you're supposed to do. I might, um, I might double-check things a bit more than I used to, or maybe I take a little longer to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm making the right choice... but I wouldn't call that avoiding, right? That's just being careful. \n\nI hope that's a good answer? I just don't think there's anything specific I'm staying away from. Is that... is that the kind of information you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my relationships? Well, I don't think so. I mean, I really try to be as supportive and present as I can be for everyone. I'd hate to think my worrying is being a burden on anyone else. \n\nMy mother and I still get along just fine, of course. I mean, I might ask her a few extra times if she's locked the doors or if she's feeling okay, but she knows I just care about her. And my friends... well, I haven't seen them quite as much lately, but that's just because work has been so busy, and I've been so focused on making sure everything at the library is running smoothly. It's not because of the worrying or anything. When we do talk, I always make sure to listen to their problems and be a good friend. \n\nI really try to keep my stress to myself so I don't bother anyone with it. I hope that's the right way to handle things? I just want to make sure I'm still being a good daughter and a good friend. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your worrying has affected your ability to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... enjoying my time with them? I think I still enjoy it. I mean, I really value the time I get to spend with my mother and my friends. They're very important to me. \n\nI suppose... well, sometimes when I'm with them, my mind might wander a little bit to work, or I might be thinking about whether I remembered to send an email, but I always try to snap right back to the conversation. I'd never want them to feel like I wasn't paying attention or that I wasn't happy to be there. I always make sure to smile and be engaged. \n\nSo, I think I still enjoy it just as much. I really try my best to be present. Is that... do you think it's normal to get a little distracted sometimes? I hope I'm not doing anything wrong by having those thoughts pop up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about being judged or evaluated by others, such as your colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... judged by others? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I try very hard to be a good colleague and a good friend, so I'd like to think people don't have a reason to judge me negatively. I always make sure to do my job well at the library and be as helpful as I can. \n\nI suppose... well, I do want people to think well of me, of course. Sometimes I might double-check my work just to be absolutely sure there aren't any mistakes, or I might think over a conversation later to make sure I didn't say anything that could be taken the wrong way. But that's just because I want to be considerate and professional, you know? I wouldn't call it worrying about being evaluated. I just like to be thorough. \n\nI think everyone wants to make a good impression, right? I hope it's okay that I care about doing a good job. Does that sound like a normal way to approach things?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is interfering with your daily life more than it is causing you emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... interfering with my daily life? Well, I really try my hardest not to let it get in the way of things, especially at work. I pride myself on being very reliable. \n\nBut I suppose... well, maybe it's a little bit of both? The emotional part is certainly tiring, but... I have noticed a few little hiccups at work lately. It's a bit embarrassing to admit, actually. I've started missing some small details at the library. Just minor things, really, but they are important, and I usually never miss them. \n\nAnd... well, there was a grant presentation recently. I actually sort of froze up during it, which is just so unlike me. I ended up having to hand off parts of it to someone else because I just couldn't find my words. I felt terrible about it. I really want to be a good leader for my team, and that little incident has made me worry about whether I can keep leading them well. \n\nI just hope my colleagues don't think I'm slacking off or anything. I really am trying my best. Do you think... is it common for stress to make you freeze up like that? I just want to make sure I'm not letting anyone down.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of any specific objects, situations, or activities that triggers your worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... specific objects or activities? No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm not afraid of anything like spiders or heights or... flying, or anything like that. It's nothing specific that I avoid. I still go everywhere I need to go and do all my normal activities. \n\nMy worries are usually just... well, just about everyday things, like I mentioned with work or making sure I'm taking care of my responsibilities. It's not triggered by a certain object or a particular place. I always try my best to just go about my day normally and get everything done. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? Is that what you meant by specific situations, or did I misunderstand?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is changing the way you think about yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... changing the way I think about myself? I... I don't think so. I mean, I still see myself as the same person I've always been—someone who tries hard, cares about her job, and wants to do the right thing. \n\nI wouldn't say the worry has changed who I am. Maybe I just feel a little more tired lately, but I still believe in being responsible and reliable. I try very hard to hold on to those values. I don't think of myself as a... a broken person or anything like that. I'm just going through a bit of a stressful patch, right? \n\nI really try to keep a positive outlook on things, even when it's tough. Is it normal to just feel like you're the same person, just... carrying a bit more on your shoulders right now? I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... thoughts that keep repeating? Like... like a broken record that I can't turn off? \n\nNo, I... I don't think I experience that. I mean, my mind can definitely get a little busy sometimes, especially when I'm going over my to-do lists to make sure I haven't missed anything at the library. But... I wouldn't say I have thoughts that just get stuck against my will, or anything strange like that. \n\nIf I need to focus on something else, I can usually shift my attention. It's really just the normal, everyday responsibilities I'm trying to manage, not... not upsetting thoughts that I can't push away. \n\nI hope that's alright? I just want to make sure I'm answering your questions correctly and giving you the right information. Am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships with others or in your ability to perform your job at the library due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say it's... significant. I mean, I still love my job and the people I work with, and I try so hard to be the best librarian and team leader I can be. My relationships are fine, really! I always make an effort to be supportive.\n\nBut... I guess at work, things have been just a tiny bit harder lately. I... I've started missing some small details here and there. Just little things, but important ones, you know? And... well, we had this grant presentation recently, and I... I sort of froze up. I actually had to hand off parts of it to someone else, which... which is so unlike me. I usually have everything perfectly prepared. \n\nIt just makes me worry a little bit about whether I'm still leading my team as well as I should be. I really don't want to let anyone down. I'm trying my best to get back on track, though! I'm sure it's just a temporary thing, right? Do you think that's normal when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is making you question your ability to do your job or take care of your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... question my ability? No, I... I really don't think so. I mean, I know I just mentioned struggling with that presentation, but... I still know I'm good at what I do. I've always taken my responsibilities very seriously. \n\nI definitely don't feel like I can't do my job or take care of things at home. I just... I guess I just worry about doing them perfectly, you know? I still handle all my daily tasks and make sure my team has what they need. I just care a lot about the quality of my work. \n\nI hope I'm not making it sound like I'm falling apart, because I'm really not. I'm still very capable and dedicated. I just have a lot on my plate right now. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining myself clearly enough for you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... overwhelming? No, I... I really wouldn't use that word. I mean, sure, it can be a little tiring sometimes, just having so much on my mind, but... but I always manage to push through it. I wouldn't say it's overwhelming at all.
